People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },

    "role": "user",

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, I guess… it’s been getting harder for me to keep my attention where it ought to be. I drive and do sales, and lately my mind keeps jumping to every little thing — the CB going off, a fuel price sign, some new idea that pops in my head — and then I miss details I normally wouldn’t miss.

It’s not like I’m trying to be careless or anything, but I’ve missed things like a client’s delivery note or forgot who I told I’d call back. It’s been going on for a few months, and I figured I should probably get it checked out.

Like a client’s delivery note, or who I said I’d call back. I don’t like admitting that, because I’m usually pretty careful with that stuff.

Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Did you have a lot of energy or difficulty sitting still when you were a child?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, honestly. I don’t remember being the kind of kid who was bouncing off the walls or anything like that.\n\nI mean, I was active, sure — most kids are — but I don’t think it stood out as a problem. Nobody ever pulled me aside about it, at least not that I can remember. Does that answer what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Did you often get into trouble in school for being overactive or restless when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Not that I remember, no. I mean, I probably got told to settle down now and then like any kid, but I don’t recall being in trouble a lot for being overactive or restless.\n\nSchool wasn’t perfect, but I don’t think that was a big issue for me. If that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Did you have any problems with paying attention or following instructions when you were in school?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not in any major way. I mean, I was probably like a lot of teenagers — sometimes I’d tune out if a class was boring, or forget a detail here and there — but I don’t remember teachers saying I couldn’t follow instructions or anything like that.\n\nI got through high school okay. So… I’d say no, not really a big problem, unless you mean something more specific?\n\nDoctor: Did you have any problems with attention or restlessness that you remember your parents or family members commenting on when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I don’t remember my parents saying I was especially restless or couldn’t pay attention or anything like that.\n\nI mean, they might’ve told me to focus or calm down once in a while, like parents do, but nothing that stood out as a real concern. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. It’s more like my attention just wanders off before I catch it, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself interrupting others or blurting out answers before they finish speaking?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in a big way, anyway. I mean, everybody cuts in once in a while, but I haven’t really had people telling me I’m doing that a lot. I try to let folks finish, especially with customers. If I do it, it’s probably just normal conversation stuff, I hope.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself fidgeting or feeling restless when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: A little, yeah. I mean, I wouldn’t say I’m bouncing off the walls or anything, but if I’m sitting too long I start feeling kind of antsy, like I ought to be doing something useful.\n\nSo I’ll find extra things to do — rewrite my route plans, check in on newer drivers after hours, fix little stuff around the house. It feels productive, mostly… but I guess I don’t relax real well when I’m supposed to just sit still. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself making impulsive decisions, like acting on something without fully thinking it through?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not in any major way. I mean, I try to think things through, especially with work and money and that kind of thing.\n\nMaybe I’ll do little spur-of-the-moment things now and then, like decide to tackle a project or run an errand without planning it out, but I wouldn’t say I’m making reckless decisions. At least I hope not. If you mean something more specific, I can try to think about it.\n\nDoctor: Have you always had trouble with attention, or is this a relatively new issue for you?\n\nPatient: It’s more of a newer thing, I think. I mean, I’ve always had normal distractions like anybody, but this feels different these past months.\n\nWhat finally made me come in was… my mind keeps jumping at everything — the CB chirping, a fuel price sign, some new idea that pops in — and then I’m missing little details I normally don’t miss. Like a client’s delivery note, or who I said I’d call back. I don’t like admitting that, because I’m usually pretty careful with that stuff.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or have trouble engaging in quiet leisure activities?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I do, a bit. I wouldn’t say I can’t sit still at all, but if I’m just supposed to relax quietly, I start feeling kind of antsy, like I should be doing something.\n\nSo I end up finding extra jobs for myself — rewriting route plans, checking in on the newer drivers after hours, fixing little things around the house. It feels useful, you know? But I suppose I don’t do “quiet leisure” very well lately. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Did you have a lot of energy or difficulty sitting still when you were a child?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, honestly. I don’t remember being the kind of kid who was bouncing off the walls or anything like that.\n\nI mean, I was active, sure — most kids are — but I don’t think it stood out as a problem. Nobody ever pulled me aside about it, at least not that I can remember. Does that answer what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Did you often get into trouble in school for being overactive or restless when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Not that I remember, no. I mean, I probably got told to settle down now and then like any kid, but I don’t recall being in trouble a lot for being overactive or restless.\n\nSchool wasn’t perfect, but I don’t think that was a big issue for me. If that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Did you have any problems with paying attention or following instructions when you were in school?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not in any major way. I mean, I was probably like a lot of teenagers — sometimes I’d tune out if a class was boring, or forget a detail here and there — but I don’t remember teachers saying I couldn’t follow instructions or anything like that.\n\nI got through high school okay. So… I’d say no, not really a big problem, unless you mean something more specific?\n\nDoctor: Did you have any problems with attention or restlessness that you remember your parents or family members commenting on when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I don’t remember my parents saying I was especially restless or couldn’t pay attention or anything like that.\n\nI mean, they might’ve told me to focus or calm down once in a while, like parents do, but nothing that stood out as a real concern. At least not that I remember. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Did you have any favorite activities as a child that you could sit and do for a long time without getting restless?\n\nPatient: Hmm… I’m not totally sure. I mean, I’m sure I had things I liked doing, but nothing really jumps out to me as, like, “I could sit for hours and hours” in a way that stands out.\n\nMaybe watching TV or playing with toys like any kid, but I don’t remember it being a big thing either way. I was pretty normal that way, I think. Does that answer what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Did you often get into physical fights or have difficulty waiting for your turn when you were a child?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I mean, I was a kid, so I’m sure there were times I got impatient or maybe had some little arguments, but I don’t remember being the kind of kid who got into physical fights a lot or couldn’t wait my turn.\n\nAt least nothing that my parents or teachers made a big issue out of, as far as I know. Is that helpful?\n\nDoctor: Did your parents or teachers ever mention that you had trouble paying attention or sitting still when you were a kid?\n\nPatient: I don’t really remember them saying that, no. Teachers didn’t call home or anything like that, as far as I know.\n\nI mean, maybe normal kid stuff—being told to pay attention once in a while—but nothing that seemed like a pattern or problem. I hope that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Did your symptoms of inattention and restlessness start during your childhood or did they develop later in life?\n\nPatient: I think it was more later in life, not really from childhood. It’s been, I’d say, about seven months or so… since my daughter moved back home and I’ve been trying to keep everything steady.\n\nI don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, but that’s when I started noticing it more. Does that answer it okay?\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms of inattention and restlessness been present for at least six months?\n\nPatient: Yes, I’d say it’s been at least six months… probably around seven months now. It started after my daughter moved back home and I was trying to keep everything steady.\n\nIt’s not constant every second or anything, but yeah, it’s been going on that long.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms of inattention and restlessness caused any problems for you at work or in your relationships?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I wouldn’t say it’s been disastrous or anything, but it has caused some issues.\n\nAt work, I’ve had to ask dispatch or clients to repeat details that I really should’ve kept straight the first time. And a couple sales follow-ups kind of slipped through because I was chasing too many things at once.\n\nAt home… that’s harder to say, but my wife and daughter seem like they’re walking on eggshells sometimes. I can tell I’m adding strain when I’m supposed to be helping. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I mean, everybody cuts in once in a while, but I haven’t really had people telling me I’m doing that a lot. I try to let folks finish, especially with customers. If I do it, it’s probably just normal conversation stuff, I hope.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself fidgeting or feeling restless when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: A little, yeah. I mean, I wouldn’t say I’m bouncing off the walls or anything, but if I’m sitting too long I start feeling kind of antsy, like I ought to be doing something useful.\n\nSo I’ll find extra things to do — rewrite my route plans, check in on newer drivers after hours, fix little stuff around the house. It feels productive, mostly… but I guess I don’t relax real well when I’m supposed to just sit still. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself making impulsive decisions, like acting on something without fully thinking it through?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not in any major way. I mean, I try to think things through, especially with work and money and that kind of thing.\n\nMaybe I’ll do little spur-of-the-moment things now and then, like decide to tackle a project or run an errand without planning it out, but I wouldn’t say I’m making reckless decisions. At least I hope not. If you mean something more specific, I can try to think about it.\n\nDoctor: Have you always had trouble with attention, or is this a relatively new issue for you?\n\nPatient: It’s more of a newer thing, I think. I mean, I’ve always had normal distractions like anybody, but this feels different these past months.\n\nWhat finally made me come in was… my mind keeps jumping at everything — the CB chirping, a fuel price sign, some new idea that pops in — and then I’m missing little details I normally don’t miss. Like a client’s delivery note, or who I said I’d call back. I don’t like admitting that, because I’m usually pretty careful with that stuff.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or have trouble engaging in quiet leisure activities?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I do, a bit. I wouldn’t say I can’t sit still at all, but if I’m just supposed to relax quietly, I start feeling kind of antsy, like I should be doing something.\n\nSo I end up finding extra jobs for myself — rewriting route plans, checking in on the newer drivers after hours, fixing little things around the house. It feels useful, you know? But I suppose I don’t do “quiet leisure” very well lately. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Did you have a lot of energy or difficulty sitting still when you were a child?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, honestly. I don’t remember being the kind of kid who was bouncing off the walls or anything like that.\n\nI mean, I was active, sure — most kids are — but I don’t think it stood out as a problem. Nobody ever pulled me aside about it, at least not that I can remember. Does that answer what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Did you often get into trouble in school for being overactive or restless when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Not that I remember, no. I mean, I probably got told to settle down now and then like any kid, but I don’t recall being in trouble a lot for being overactive or restless.\n\nSchool wasn’t perfect, but I don’t think that was a big issue for me. If that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Did you have any problems with paying attention or following instructions when you were in school?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not in any major way. I mean, I was probably like a lot of teenagers — sometimes I’d tune out if a class was boring, or forget a detail here and there — but I don’t remember teachers saying I couldn’t follow instructions or anything like that.\n\nI got through high school okay. So… I’d say no, not really a big problem, unless you mean something more specific?\n\nDoctor: Did you have any problems with attention or restlessness that you remember your parents or family members commenting on when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I don’t remember my parents saying I was especially restless or couldn’t pay attention or anything like that.\n\nI mean, they might’ve told me to focus or calm down once in a while, like parents do, but nothing that stood out as a real concern. At least not that I remember. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Did you have any favorite activities as a child that you could sit and do for a long time without getting restless?\n\nPatient: Hmm… I’m not totally sure. I mean, I’m sure I had things I liked doing, but nothing really jumps out to me as, like, “I could sit for hours and hours” in a way that stands out.\n\nMaybe watching TV or playing with toys like any kid, but I don’t remember it being a big thing either way. I was pretty normal that way, I think. Does that answer what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Did you often get into physical fights or have difficulty waiting for your turn when you were a child?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I mean, I was a kid, so I’m sure there were times I got impatient or maybe had some little arguments, but I don’t remember being the kind of kid who got into physical fights a lot or couldn’t wait my turn.\n\nAt least nothing that my parents or teachers made a big issue out of, as far as I know. Is that helpful?\n\nDoctor: Did your parents or teachers ever mention that you had trouble paying attention or sitting still when you were a kid?\n\nPatient: I don’t really remember them saying that, no. Teachers didn’t call home or anything like that, as far as I know.\n\nI mean, maybe normal kid stuff—being told to pay attention once in a while—but nothing that seemed like a pattern or problem. I hope that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Did your symptoms of inattention and restlessness start during your childhood or did they develop later in life?\n\nPatient: I think it was more later in life, not really from childhood. It’s been, I’d say, about seven months or so… since my daughter moved back home and I’ve been trying to keep everything steady.\n\nI don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, but that’s when I started noticing it more. Does that answer it okay?\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms of inattention and restlessness been present for at least six months?\n\nPatient: Yes, I’d say it’s been at least six months… probably around seven months now. It started after my daughter moved back home and I was trying to keep everything steady.\n\nIt’s not constant every second or anything, but yeah, it’s been going on that long.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms of inattention and restlessness caused any problems for you at work or in your relationships?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I wouldn’t say it’s been disastrous or anything, but it has caused some issues.\n\nAt work, I’ve had to ask dispatch or clients to repeat details that I really should’ve kept straight the first time. And a couple sales follow-ups kind of slipped through because I was chasing too many things at once.\n\nAt home… that’s harder to say, but my wife and daughter seem like they’re walking on eggshells sometimes. I can tell I’m adding strain when I’m supposed to be helping. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms of inattention and restlessness been present for at least 6 months consistently?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so… I mean, it hasn’t been every minute of every day, but it’s been going on pretty steadily for about seven months now, since my daughter moved back home and I started trying to keep everything steady.\n\nSo yes, at least six months. I drive and do sales, and lately my mind keeps jumping to every little thing — the CB going off, a fuel price sign, some new idea that pops in my head — and then I miss details I normally wouldn’t miss.\n\nIt’s not like I’m trying to be careless or anything, but I’ve missed things like a client’s delivery note or forgot who I told I’d call back. It’s been going on for a few months, and I figured I should probably get it checked out.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling anxious or stressed when these attention problems happen?\n\nPatient: I mean… not especially, I don’t think. I wouldn’t say I’m sitting there anxious or panicky when it happens. Work can be work, sure, but it doesn’t feel like stress is what’s causing it. It’s more like my attention just wanders off before I catch it, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself interrupting others or blurting out answers before they finish speaking?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in a big way, anyway. I mean, everybody cuts in once in a while, but I haven’t really had people telling me I’m doing that a lot. I try to let folks finish, especially with customers. If I do it, it’s probably just normal conversation stuff, I hope.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself fidgeting or feeling restless when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: A little, yeah. I mean, I wouldn’t say I’m bouncing off the walls or anything, but if I’m sitting too long I start feeling kind of antsy, like I ought to be doing something useful.\n\nSo I’ll find extra things to do — rewrite my route plans, check in on newer drivers after hours, fix little stuff around the house. It feels productive, mostly… but I guess I don’t relax real well when I’m supposed to just sit still. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself making impulsive decisions, like acting on something without fully thinking it through?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not in any major way. I mean, I try to think things through, especially with work and money and that kind of thing.\n\nMaybe I’ll do little spur-of-the-moment things now and then, like decide to tackle a project or run an errand without planning it out, but I wouldn’t say I’m making reckless decisions. At least I hope not. If you mean something more specific, I can try to think about it.\n\nDoctor: Have you always had trouble with attention, or is this a relatively new issue for you?\n\nPatient: It’s more of a newer thing, I think. I mean, I’ve always had normal distractions like anybody, but this feels different these past months.\n\nWhat finally made me come in was… my mind keeps jumping at everything — the CB chirping, a fuel price sign, some new idea that pops in — and then I’m missing little details I normally don’t miss. Like a client’s delivery note, or who I said I’d call back. I don’t like admitting that, because I’m usually pretty careful with that stuff.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or have trouble engaging in quiet leisure activities?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I do, a bit. I wouldn’t say I can’t sit still at all, but if I’m just supposed to relax quietly, I start feeling kind of antsy, like I should be doing something.\n\nSo I end up finding extra jobs for myself — rewriting route plans, checking in on the newer drivers after hours, fixing little things around the house. It feels useful, you know? But I suppose I don’t do “quiet leisure” very well lately. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Did you have a lot of energy or difficulty sitting still when you were a child?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, honestly. I don’t remember being the kind of kid who was bouncing off the walls or anything like that.\n\nI mean, I was active, sure — most kids are — but I don’t think it stood out as a problem. Nobody ever pulled me aside about it, at least not that I can remember. Does that answer what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Did you often get into trouble in school for being overactive or restless when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Not that I remember, no. I mean, I probably got told to settle down now and then like any kid, but I don’t recall being in trouble a lot for being overactive or restless.\n\nSchool wasn’t perfect, but I don’t think that was a big issue for me. If that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Did you have any problems with paying attention or following instructions when you were in school?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not in any major way. I mean, I was probably like a lot of teenagers — sometimes I’d tune out if a class was boring, or forget a detail here and there — but I don’t remember teachers saying I couldn’t follow instructions or anything like that.\n\nI got through high school okay. So… I’d say no, not really a big problem, unless you mean something more specific?\n\nDoctor: Did you have any problems with attention or restlessness that you remember your parents or family members commenting on when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I don’t remember my parents saying I was especially restless or couldn’t pay attention or anything like that.\n\nI mean, they might’ve told me to focus or calm down once in a while, like parents do, but nothing that stood out as a real concern. At least not that I remember. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Did you have any favorite activities as a child that you could sit and do for a long time without getting restless?\n\nPatient: Hmm… I’m not totally sure. I mean, I’m sure I had things I liked doing, but nothing really jumps out to me as, like, “I could sit for hours and hours” in a way that stands out.\n\nMaybe watching TV or playing with toys like any kid, but I don’t remember it being a big thing either way. I was pretty normal that way, I think. Does that answer what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Did you often get into physical fights or have difficulty waiting for your turn when you were a child?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I mean, I was a kid, so I’m sure there were times I got impatient or maybe had some little arguments, but I don’t remember being the kind of kid who got into physical fights a lot or couldn’t wait my turn.\n\nAt least nothing that my parents or teachers made a big issue out of, as far as I know. Is that helpful?\n\nDoctor: Did your parents or teachers ever mention that you had trouble paying attention or sitting still when you were a kid?\n\nPatient: I don’t really remember them saying that, no. Teachers didn’t call home or anything like that, as far as I know.\n\nI mean, maybe normal kid stuff—being told to pay attention once in a while—but nothing that seemed like a pattern or problem. I hope that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Did your symptoms of inattention and restlessness start during your childhood or did they develop later in life?\n\nPatient: I think it was more later in life, not really from childhood. It’s been, I’d say, about seven months or so… since my daughter moved back home and I’ve been trying to keep everything steady.\n\nI don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, but that’s when I started noticing it more. Does that answer it okay?\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms of inattention and restlessness been present for at least six months?\n\nPatient: Yes, I’d say it’s been at least six months… probably around seven months now. It started after my daughter moved back home and I was trying to keep everything steady.\n\nIt’s not constant every second or anything, but yeah, it’s been going on that long.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms of inattention and restlessness caused any problems for you at work or in your relationships?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I wouldn’t say it’s been disastrous or anything, but it has caused some issues.\n\nAt work, I’ve had to ask dispatch or clients to repeat details that I really should’ve kept straight the first time. And a couple sales follow-ups kind of slipped through because I was chasing too many things at once.\n\nAt home… that’s harder to say, but my wife and daughter seem like they’re walking on eggshells sometimes. I can tell I’m adding strain when I’m supposed to be helping. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms of inattention and restlessness been present for at least 6 months consistently?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so… I mean, it hasn’t been every minute of every day, but it’s been going on pretty steadily for about seven months now, since my daughter moved back home and I started trying to keep everything steady.\n\nSo yes, at least six months. I hope I’m answering that clearly.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced significant symptoms of inattention and restlessness in multiple settings, such as at work and at home?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it has shown up in both places. At work, I’ve had to ask dispatch or clients to repeat details I should’ve kept track of, and a couple of my sales follow-ups slipped through because I was kind of chasing too many things at once.\n\nAnd at home… I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, but my wife and daughter have seemed like they’re walking on eggshells around me sometimes. I can tell I’m adding strain when I’m supposed to be helping. Is that what you mean by multiple settings?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says.   },

    "role": "user",

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not really sure, honestly. I don’t remember being a particularly fidgety kid, but that was a long time ago, and I don’t want to give you the wrong answer.

I mean, I probably got told to settle down now and then like any kid, but I don’t recall being in trouble a lot for being overactive or restless.\n\nSchool wasn’t perfect, but I don’t think that was a big issue for me. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Did you have any favorite activities as a child that you could sit and do for a long time without getting restless?\n\nPatient: Hmm… I’m not totally sure. I was pretty normal that way, I think. Does that answer what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Did you often get into physical fights or have difficulty waiting for your turn when you were a child?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. Is that helpful?\n\nDoctor: Did your parents or teachers ever mention that you had trouble paying attention or sitting still when you were a kid?\n\nPatient: I don’t really remember them saying that, no.
